### dead draw
`wikipedia:glossary:dead draw` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

dead draw: A drawn position in which neither player has any realistic chance to win. In the strict sense, dead draw may refer to a position in which it is impossible for either player to win (such as insufficient material). In a broader sense, it may refer to a simple, lifeless position that would require a major blunder before either side would have a chance to win.

### dead position
`wikipedia:glossary:dead position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

dead position: A position where neither player can mate the opponent's king with any series of legal moves (e.g. knight and king against a bare king). This position is drawn.

### decoy
`wikipedia:glossary:decoy` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

decoy: This is a tactic used to lure an enemy man away from its defensive position. See also deflection and attraction.

### defense
`wikipedia:glossary:defense` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

defense: 1. A move or plan to meet the opponent's attack. 2. Part of the name of openings played by Black; e.g. the Scandinavian Defense, King's Indian Defense, English Defense, etc.

### deflection
`wikipedia:glossary:deflection` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

deflection: A decoy tactic that involves luring an enemy piece away from a good square; typically, away from a square on which it defends another piece or threat. Deflection is thus closely related to overloading. See also attraction.

### demonstration board
`wikipedia:glossary:demonstration board` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

demonstration board: A large standing chess board used to analyze a game or show a game in progress. Johann Löwenthal invented the demonstration board in 1857.

### descriptive notation
`wikipedia:glossary:descriptive notation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

descriptive notation: A system of recording chess moves, used primarily in the English- and Spanish-speaking countries until the 1980s. Descriptive notation is based on natural language descriptions of chess moves rendered in abbreviated form, for example "pawn to queen's bishop's fourth" is rendered as "P-QB4". Now replaced by the standard algebraic notation.

### desperado
`wikipedia:glossary:desperado` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

desperado: A piece that seems determined to give itself up, typically to bring about stalemate or perpetual check. Also an en prise or trapped piece that sacrifices itself for the maximum compensation possible.

### development
`wikipedia:glossary:development` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

development: The movement of non-pawn pieces in the opening from their original squares to squares where they can be more active. Development of one's pieces is one of the objectives of the opening phase of the game.

### diagonal
`wikipedia:glossary:diagonal` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

diagonal: A line of squares of the same color touching corner to corner, along which a queen or bishop moves.

### dirty flag
`wikipedia:glossary:dirty flag` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

dirty flag: A scenario in which a player having a losing position on the board wins by virtue of the opponent exceeding the time control. It typically occurs in faster formats, such as blitz or bullet, where a player prioritizes making rapid (often sub-optimal) moves to exhaust the opponent's remaining seconds rather than seeking a tactical or positional advantage. See also flag.

### discovered attack
`wikipedia:glossary:discovered attack` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

discovered attack: An attack made by a queen, rook, or bishop when another piece or pawn moves out of its way.

### discovered check
`wikipedia:glossary:discovered check` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

discovered check: A discovered attack to the king. This occurs when a player moves a piece, resulting in another piece putting their opponent's king in check.

### diversionary sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:diversionary sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

diversionary sacrifice: Especially in the middlegame, the sacrifice of a decoy piece.

### domination
`wikipedia:glossary:domination` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

domination: From endgame studies, control of all movement squares of an enemy piece.

### double attack
`wikipedia:glossary:double attack` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

double attack: Two attacks made with one move: these attacks may be made by the same piece (in which case it is a fork); or by different pieces, for example in a discovered attack when the moved piece also makes a threat.

### double check
`wikipedia:glossary:double check` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

double check: A check delivered by two pieces at the same time. A double check necessarily involves a discovered check. By its nature a double check cannot be met by interposing a defending piece in a line of attack, or by capturing an attacker; when subjected to a double check, the attacked king must move. This makes the double check especially powerful as an attacking tactic.

### doubled pawns
`wikipedia:glossary:doubled pawns` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

doubled pawns: Two pawns of the same color on the same file; generally considered a weakness due to their inability to defend each other.

### doubled rooks
`wikipedia:glossary:doubled rooks` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

doubled rooks: A powerful configuration in which a player's two rooks are placed on the same file or rank with no other men between them. They defend each other and attack along the shared file or rank, as well as two additional ranks or files. The configuration can be especially decisive in the endgame.

### double fianchetto
`wikipedia:glossary:double fianchetto` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

double fianchetto: A player's king bishop and queen bishop have both been fianchettoed.

### draw
`wikipedia:glossary:draw` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

draw: A game that ends without victory for either player. Most drawn games are draws by agreement. The other ways that a game can end in a draw are by stalemate, by a dead position, by the threefold repetition rule, by the fifty-move rule, by the fivefold repetition rule, and by the seventy-five-move rule. A position is said to be a draw (or a "drawn position" or "theoretical draw") if either player can, through correct play, eventually force the game into a position where the game must end in a draw, regardless of the moves made by the other player. A draw is usually scored as ½ point, although in some matches only wins are counted and draws are ignored.

### draw by agreement
`wikipedia:glossary:draw by agreement` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

draw by agreement: A game that is ended by both players' accepting a draw. See also resign.

### draw death
`wikipedia:glossary:draw death` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

draw death: Hypothetical scenario whereby elite-level chess players, aided by modern computer analysis, become so good that they never make mistakes, leading to endless drawn games (since chess is widely believed to be drawn with best play from both sides).

### drawing line
`wikipedia:glossary:drawing line` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

drawing line: An opening variation that commonly ends in a draw.

### drawing weapon
`wikipedia:glossary:drawing weapon` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

drawing weapon: An opening line played with the intent of drawing the game.

### drawish
`wikipedia:glossary:drawish` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

drawish: An adjective describing a position or game that is likely to end in a draw.

### draw odds
`wikipedia:glossary:draw odds` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

draw odds: A type of chess handicap where one player (Black in an Armageddon game) has only to draw in order to win the match.

### draw offer
`wikipedia:glossary:draw offer` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

draw offer: A proposal by a player to the opponent that the game be draw by agreement.

### dynamism
`wikipedia:glossary:dynamism` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

dynamism: A style of play in which the activity of the pieces is favored over more positional considerations, even to the point of accepting permanent structural or spatial weaknesses. Dynamism stemmed from the teachings of the Hypermodern school and challenged the dogma found in more classical teachings, such as those put forward by Wilhelm Steinitz and Siegbert Tarrasch.

### eat
`wikipedia:glossary:eat` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

eat: [slang] To remove the opponent's piece or pawn from the board by taking it with one's own piece or pawn. Synonym: capture.

### ECF
`wikipedia:glossary:ECF` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

ECF: The English Chess Federation (ECF) is the governing chess organisation in England and is one of the federations of FIDE. It was known as the British Chess Federation (BCF) until 2005 when it was renamed.

### ECO
`wikipedia:glossary:ECO` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

ECO: Abbr. for the Encyclopaedia of Chess Openings, a standard 5-volume chess opening reference. Also ECO code, a classification system for openings that assigns an alphanumeric code from A00 to E99 to each opening.

### ECU
`wikipedia:glossary:ECU` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

ECU: The European Chess Union (ECU) is the continental association for chess in Europe.

### edge
`wikipedia:glossary:edge` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

edge: A small but meaningful advantage in position against one's opponent. It is often said that White has an edge in the starting position, since White moves first (see First-move advantage in chess). cf. initiative.

### eighth rank
`wikipedia:glossary:eighth rank` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

eighth rank: The rank on which pawns promote (rank eight for White; rank one for Black). Also called last rank.

### Elo rating system
`wikipedia:glossary:Elo rating system` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Elo rating system: The Elo rating system, named after the Hungarian-American statistician and chess player Arpad Elo, is a method for calculating the relative skill levels of chess players based on their results in competition. FIDE adopted the system in 1970, initially publishing ratings annually. Over the years the list has been published with increasing frequency, and since 2012 it has been published monthly. The majority of national chess federations have their own rating system for use in domestic competition, usually based on the Elo system.

### endgame
`wikipedia:glossary:endgame` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

endgame: The third and last phase of the game, when there are few pieces left on the board. The endgame follows the middlegame.

### endgame tablebase
`wikipedia:glossary:endgame tablebase` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

endgame tablebase: A computerized database of endgames with a small number of pieces, providing perfect play for both players, and thus completely solving those endgames. As of 2012, tablebases have been calculated for all positions with up to seven pieces.

### en passant ("e.p.")
`wikipedia:glossary:en passant ("e.p.")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

en passant ("e.p."): [from French, 'in the act of passing'] The rule that allows a pawn that has just advanced two squares to be captured by an enemy pawn that is on the same rank and adjacent file. The pawn can be taken as if it had advanced only one square. Capturing en passant is possible only on the next move.

### en prise
`wikipedia:glossary:en prise` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

en prise: [from French, 'in a position to be taken', often italicized] En prise describes a piece or pawn exposed to a material-winning capture by the opponent. This is either a hanging piece, an undefended pawn, a piece attacked by a less valuable attacker, or a piece or pawn defended insufficiently. For instance, 1.e4 Nf6 2.Nf3? leaves White's e-pawn en prise.

### epaulette mate
`wikipedia:glossary:epaulette mate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

epaulette mate: A checkmate position where the king is blocked on both sides by its own rooks.

### EPD
`wikipedia:glossary:EPD` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

EPD: Abbr. for Extended Position Description.

### equalize
`wikipedia:glossary:equalize` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

equalize: Or equalise. To reach a position where the players have equal chances of winning, referred to as equality, or a position that is equal, or a position that is balanced. In the opening, because White has the advantage of the first move, the immediate goal for Black is to achieve equality.

### evaluation
`wikipedia:glossary:evaluation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

evaluation: Or simply eval. The analysis of a position. A computer or engine evaluation is a means of assigning a number value to a position, based not on intelligence, but on algorithms, which vary from engine to engine and depend on engine strength. Engine evaluations have foibles and imperfections even when functioning as designed. If an engine describes a position as +2.50, the plus sign ("+") indicates the position is favorable to White; a minus sign ("−") indicates the position is favorable to Black. The number can correspond to the approximate value of pieces, although engines use other factors besides material. The notation +2.50 indicates that White is ahead by two and one-half pawns. The notation +M4 (or sometimes #4) indicates that White can force checkmate in four moves. cf. analysis.

### exchange
`wikipedia:glossary:exchange` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

exchange: To swap or trade pieces by capture. Usually the pieces are of equal value (i.e., rook for rook, knight for knight, etc.), or of bishop for knight (two pieces that are considered approximately equal in value). Although some writers define an exchange to always be of equal value, most writers do not.

### exchange, the
`wikipedia:glossary:exchange, the` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

exchange, the: The advantage of a rook over a minor piece (knight or bishop). The player who captures a rook for a minor piece is said to have "won the exchange", the player who has lost the rook has "lost the exchange". An exchange sacrifice is giving up a rook for a minor piece.

### exchange variation
`wikipedia:glossary:exchange variation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

exchange variation: This is a type of opening in which there is an early, voluntary exchange of pawns or pieces.

### exhibition
`wikipedia:glossary:exhibition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

exhibition: Chess games played for the public in various formats and for various purposes, often to promote the game, or a particular match or player, or as a fundraiser. An exhibition may pit two masters against each other, and normally use chess clocks. In a simultaneous exhibition, one player takes on a number of opponents at once, and it is often not timed. A blindfold exhibition is the same but more challenging, since the exhibitor plays without seeing the boards.

### expanded center
`wikipedia:glossary:expanded center` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

expanded center: The central sixteen squares of the chessboard.

### exposed king
`wikipedia:glossary:exposed king` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

exposed king: A king lacking pawns to shield it from enemy attack.

### Extended Position Description ("EPD")
`wikipedia:glossary:Extended Position Description ("EPD")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Extended Position Description ("EPD"): A Forsyth–Edwards Notation derivative format that contains the position on the chessboard, but not the game. It is primarily used to test chess engines.

### family fork
`wikipedia:glossary:family fork` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

family fork: A knight fork that simultaneously attacks the enemy king (giving check), queen, and possibly other pieces. Also known as a family check.

### FAN
`wikipedia:glossary:FAN` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

FAN: Abbr. for figurine algebraic notation, which substitutes symbols for letters to represent piece names (e.g. ♘f3 instead of Nf3).

### fast chess
`wikipedia:glossary:fast chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fast chess: A form of chess in which both sides are given less time to make their moves than under the normal tournament time controls. See also rapid chess, blitz chess, and bullet chess.

### fianchetto
`wikipedia:glossary:fianchetto` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fianchetto: [from Italian, 'little flank'] To develop a bishop to the board's longest diagonal on the file of the adjacent knight (b2 or g2 for White; b7 or g7 for Black). The fianchetto of both bishops by a player is called a double fianchetto. Less common is to develop a bishop to the rook's file (a3 or h3 for White; a6 or h6 for Black), called extended fianchetto. The Italian word is pronounced or in English, while its name sounds like [fjaŋ.ˈket.to] in Italian.

### FICGS
`wikipedia:glossary:FICGS` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

FICGS: Abbr. for Free Internet Correspondence Games Server.

### FICGS Grandmaster
`wikipedia:glossary:FICGS Grandmaster` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

FICGS Grandmaster: A correspondence chess title calculated by the FICGS (Free Internet Correspondence Games Server) organization.

### FIDE
`wikipedia:glossary:FIDE` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

FIDE: Abbr. for French: Fédération Internationale des Échecs ('International Chess Federation'), the primary international chess organizing and governing body. The abbreviated name FIDE is nearly always used in place of the full name in French.

### FIDE Master ("FM")
`wikipedia:glossary:FIDE Master ("FM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

FIDE Master ("FM"): A chess title ranking below International Master.

### fifty-move rule
`wikipedia:glossary:fifty-move rule` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fifty-move rule: A draw may be claimed if no capture or pawn move has occurred in the last fifty moves by either side. This is related to the seventy-five-move rule, which mandates a draw after seventy-five such moves.

### file
`wikipedia:glossary:file` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

file: A column of squares of the chessboard. A specific file can be named either using its position in algebraic notation, a–h, or by using its position in descriptive notation. For example, "f-file" and "king bishop file" both denote the squares f1–f8 (or KB1–KB8 in descriptive notation).

### fingerfehler
`wikipedia:glossary:fingerfehler` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fingerfehler: [from German, 'finger mistake'] An error caused by unthinkingly touching the wrong piece or releasing a piece on the wrong square, forcing the player to move that piece in accordance with the touch-move rule.

### first board
`wikipedia:glossary:first board` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

first board: In team chess, the player who is assigned to face the strongest opponents. Also called top board and board one. Second board faces the next strongest players, followed by third board, and so on. Generally board assignments must be made before the competition begins. Players may not switch boards, although reserve players are often allowed as substitutes.

### first-move advantage
`wikipedia:glossary:first-move advantage` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

first-move advantage: The slight (by most accounts) advantage that White has by virtue of moving first.

### first player
`wikipedia:glossary:first player` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

first player: The expression "the first player" is sometimes used to refer to White.

### Fischer delay
`wikipedia:glossary:Fischer delay` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Fischer delay: A time control method with time delay, invented by Bobby Fischer. When it becomes a player's turn to move, the delay is added to the player's remaining time.

### Fischerandom
`wikipedia:glossary:Fischerandom` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Fischerandom: The name given by Bobby Fischer for the variant of chess he invented. See Chess960.

### fish
`wikipedia:glossary:fish` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fish: [derogatory slang] A weak or easily defeated player or players. See also woodpusher.

### fivefold repetition
`wikipedia:glossary:fivefold repetition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fivefold repetition: A game is drawn if the same position occurs five times with the same player to move and with each player having the choice of the same set of moves each time, including the right to capture en passant and the right to castle. The threefold repetition rule operates on a similar principle but is invoked at a player's discretion instead of automatically.

### flag
`wikipedia:glossary:flag` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

flag: Part of an analogue chess clock, usually red, that indicates when the minute hand passes the hour. To "flag" someone means winning the game on the basis of the opponent exceeding the time control.

### flag-fall
`wikipedia:glossary:flag-fall` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

flag-fall: The event when the allotted time of a player has just expired; the player has run out of time.

### flank
`wikipedia:glossary:flank` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

flank: The queenside a-, b-, and c-files; or the kingside f-, g-, and h-files. Distinguished from the center d- and e-files. Also called wing.

### flank opening
`wikipedia:glossary:flank opening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

flank opening: An opening played by White and typified by play on one or both flanks.

### flight square
`wikipedia:glossary:flight square` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

flight square: A square to which a piece can move, that allows it to escape attack. Also called escape square. See also luft.

### Fool's mate
`wikipedia:glossary:Fool's mate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Fool's mate: The shortest possible chess game ending in mate: 1.f3 e5 2.g4 Qh4# (or minor variations on this).

### forced mate
`wikipedia:glossary:forced mate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

forced mate: A sequence of two or more moves culminating in checkmate that the opponent cannot prevent.

### forced move
`wikipedia:glossary:forced move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

forced move: The only legal move in a position, or the only move which does not result in an immediate loss or serious disadvantage. cf. forcing move.

### forced win
`wikipedia:glossary:forced win` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

forced win: A win guaranteed by a series of forcing moves.

### forcing move
`wikipedia:glossary:forcing move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

forcing move: A move that presents a threat and limits the opponent's responses. cf. forced move.

### forfeit
`wikipedia:glossary:forfeit` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

forfeit: Refers to losing the game by breaking rules, by absence or by exceeding the time control (forfeit on time).

### fork
`wikipedia:glossary:fork` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fork: A simultaneous attack by a single piece on two (or more) of the opponent's pieces (or other direct target, such as a mate threat). When the attacker is a knight the tactic is often specifically called a knight fork. Some sources state that only a knight can give a fork and that the term double attack is correct when another piece is involved, but this usage is rare.

### Forsyth–Edwards Notation ("FEN")
`wikipedia:glossary:Forsyth–Edwards Notation ("FEN")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Forsyth–Edwards Notation ("FEN"): A standard notation for describing a particular board position of a chess game. The purpose of FEN notation is to provide all the necessary information to restart a game from a particular position.

### fortress
`wikipedia:glossary:fortress` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

fortress: In endgame theory, a fortress is an impenetrable position which, if obtained by the side with a material disadvantage, may result in a draw due to the stronger side's inability to make progress.

### frame
`wikipedia:glossary:frame` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

frame: A square region of the board enclosing another region not part of the given frame, akin to a picture frame. Also referred to as a ring. The outer frame consists of the 28 squares along the edge of the board, the middle frame consists of the 20 squares just inside the outer frame, and the inner frame consists of the 12 squares just inside the middle frame. The notion of the frame may be expanded to include the center itself as the innermost frame. The mobility of pieces is closely related to the frame on which they stand. In general, a piece closer to the center has greater freedom of movement than a piece closer to the edge of the board.

### friendly game
`wikipedia:glossary:friendly game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

friendly game: A game that is not played as part of a match, tournament, or exhibition. Often the game is not timed, but if a chess clock is used, rapid time controls are common. The term refers only to the circumstances in which the game is played, not the relationship between the players or the intensity of the competition. Also called casual game and informal game.

### frontier line
`wikipedia:glossary:frontier line` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

frontier line: An imaginary line dividing the board into two halves, passing between the fourth and fifth ranks. The frontier line separates White's side of the board from Black's side. Coined by Aron Nimzowitsch.

### gambit
`wikipedia:glossary:gambit` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

gambit: A sacrifice (usually of a pawn) used to gain an early advantage in space or time in the opening. See also countergambit.

### game score
`wikipedia:glossary:game score` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

game score: Often shortened to score. The record of a game in some form of notation, usually algebraic notation. In over-the-board tournaments, the game score is recorded on a score sheet.

### gardez
`wikipedia:glossary:gardez` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

gardez: [from French: gardez la reine!, 'Protect the Queen!'] An announcement to the opponent that their queen is under direct attack, similar to the announcement of "check". This warning was customary until the early 20th century.

### God
`wikipedia:glossary:God` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

God: Metaphorical; a hypothetical player who always plays perfectly.

### good bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:good bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

good bishop: A bishop that has greater mobility, because the player's own pawns are on squares of the opposite color to that of the bishop. cf. bad bishop.

### Grandmaster ("GM")
`wikipedia:glossary:Grandmaster ("GM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Grandmaster ("GM"): The highest title a chess player can attain (besides World Champion). Awarded by FIDE, the title is valid for life unless exceptional circumstances (such as cheating) occur.

### grandmaster draw
`wikipedia:glossary:grandmaster draw` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

grandmaster draw: A game in which the players agree to a quick draw. Originally it referred to such games between grandmasters, but the term can now refer to any such game.

### Greek gift sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:Greek gift sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Greek gift sacrifice: A typical sacrifice of a bishop by White playing Bxh7+ or by Black playing ...Bxh2+ against a castled king to initiate a mating attack. Also known as the classical bishop sacrifice.

### half-open file
`wikipedia:glossary:half-open file` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

half-open file: A file on which only one player has pawns. Also called semi-open file.

### hanging
`wikipedia:glossary:hanging` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

hanging: Unprotected and exposed to capture. A hanging piece may also be said to be en prise.

### hanging pawns
`wikipedia:glossary:hanging pawns` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

hanging pawns: Two pawns of the same color on adjacent files, with no pawns of the same color on the files to either side of them.

### Harry
`wikipedia:glossary:Harry` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Harry: A nickname for the h-pawn, sometimes occurring in the expression, "Harry the h-pawn".

### hauptturnier
`wikipedia:glossary:hauptturnier` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

hauptturnier: German word that is freely translated as 'premiere tournament'. In the early part of the 20th century, it was necessary for the ambitious European amateur to win a succession of prizes in small tournaments, to progress to a higher level of competition. The creation of the hauptturnier enabled the process to become more formalized, and they became a regular feature of the major German chess congresses. Winning such an event conferred the title of 'Master of the German Chess Federation', and this, in turn, could be used to gain admittance to prestigious international tournaments. Some of the best players in chess history, such as Emanuel Lasker and Siegbert Tarrasch, secured their Master titles and advanced their chess careers in this way.

### hole
`wikipedia:glossary:hole` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

hole: A square that is inside or near a player's territory that cannot be controlled by a pawn. It is a gap in a player's pawn configuration, and especially dangerous when the hole is close to the center or near the king. A knight landing on a hole may be part of an attack. An example of a hole is the e4-square in the Stonewall Attack.

### home rank
`wikipedia:glossary:home rank` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

home rank: The rank on which the pieces stand in the starting position (rank one for White; rank eight for Black). Also called back rank and first rank.

### Horwitz bishops
`wikipedia:glossary:Horwitz bishops` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Horwitz bishops: A player's light-square bishop and dark-square bishop placed so that they occupy adjacent diagonals, creating a potent attack. Also called raking bishops, and sometimes Harrwitz bishops.

### human move
`wikipedia:glossary:human move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

human move: A move a human would make, as opposed to the kind of move that only a computer would make.

### Hutton pairing
`wikipedia:glossary:Hutton pairing` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Hutton pairing: A pairing technique invented in 1921 by George Dickson Hutton for matching teams of players in which only one game is required per player. It has been used regularly for correspondence team events and for matches between many teams conducted on one day. Also called jamboree pairing.

### hypermodernism
`wikipedia:glossary:hypermodernism` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

hypermodernism: A school of thought that prefers controlling the center with pieces from the flanks as opposed to occupying it directly with pawns. Two major proponents of hypermodernism were Richard Réti and Aron Nimzowitsch. See also classical.

### ICCF
`wikipedia:glossary:ICCF` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

ICCF: Abbr. for International Correspondence Chess Federation.

### IGM
`wikipedia:glossary:IGM` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

IGM: Abbr. for the older term International Grandmaster. The modern usage is Grandmaster (GM).

### illegal move
`wikipedia:glossary:illegal move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

illegal move: A move that is not permitted by the rules of chess. An illegal move discovered during the course of a game must be corrected.

### illegal position
`wikipedia:glossary:illegal position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

illegal position: 1. A position in a game that is a consequence of an illegal move or an incorrect starting position 2. A position that is impossible to reach by any sequence of legal moves.

### imbalance
`wikipedia:glossary:imbalance` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

imbalance: Any difference between the positions of White and Black. An imbalanced position is one where White and Black both have unique advantages. Conversely, a balanced position may be drawish.

### inaccuracy
`wikipedia:glossary:inaccuracy` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

inaccuracy: A move that is not the best, but not as bad as a mistake or blunder.

### increment
`wikipedia:glossary:increment` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

increment: Refers to the amount of time added to each player's time before each move. For instance, rapid chess might be played with "25 minutes plus 10 second per move increment", meaning that each player starts with 25 minutes on their clock, and this increments by 10 seconds after (or before) each move, usually using the Fischer Delay method. See also Time control § Increment and delay methods.

### Indian bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:Indian bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Indian bishop: A fianchettoed bishop, characteristic of Indian defenses, the King's Indian and the Queen's Indian.

### Indian Defense
`wikipedia:glossary:Indian Defense` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Indian Defense: An opening that begins 1.d4 Nf6. Originally used to describe queen's pawn defenses involving the fianchetto of one or both black bishops; now used to describe all Black defenses after 1.d4 Nf6 that do not transpose into the Queen's Gambit.

### initiative
`wikipedia:glossary:initiative` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

initiative: The ability to make attacking moves, and force the course of play. It is an aspect of time. The attacking player has the initiative, and the defending player attempts to seize it.

### insufficient material
`wikipedia:glossary:insufficient material` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

insufficient material: An endgame scenario in which all pawns have been captured, and one side has only its king remaining while the other has only its king, a king plus a knight, or a king plus a bishop. A king plus bishop versus a king plus bishop with the bishops on the same color is also a draw, since neither side can checkmate, regardless of play. Situations where checkmate is possible only if the inferior side blunders are covered by the fifty-move rule. See also Draw (chess) § Draws in all games.

### interference
`wikipedia:glossary:interference` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

interference: The interruption of the line or diagonal between an attacked piece and its defender by interposing a piece.

### International Arbiter ("IA")
`wikipedia:glossary:International Arbiter ("IA")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

International Arbiter ("IA"): A tournament official who arbitrates disputes and performs other duties such as keeping the score when players are under time pressure.

### International Correspondence Chess Federation
`wikipedia:glossary:International Correspondence Chess Federation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

International Correspondence Chess Federation: Organization founded in 1951 to replace the International Correspondence Chess Association (ICCA).

### International Grandmaster ("IGM")
`wikipedia:glossary:International Grandmaster ("IGM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

International Grandmaster ("IGM"): The original name of the FIDE title, now simply called Grandmaster ("GM")

### International Master ("IM")
`wikipedia:glossary:International Master ("IM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

International Master ("IM"): A chess title that ranks below Grandmaster but above FIDE Master.

### International Woman Master
`wikipedia:glossary:International Woman Master` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

International Woman Master: Obsolete name for Woman International Master.

### Internet chess server ("ICS")
`wikipedia:glossary:Internet chess server ("ICS")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Internet chess server ("ICS"): An external server that provides the facility to play, discuss, and view chess over the Internet.

### interpose
`wikipedia:glossary:interpose` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

interpose: To move a piece between an attacking piece and its target, blocking the line of attack. Interposing is not possible if the attacker is a knight, king, or pawn; it is possible only when the attacker is a rook, bishop, or queen. Interposing a piece is one of three possible responses to a check.

### Interzonal tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:Interzonal tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Interzonal tournament: A tournament organized by the FIDE starting from the 1950s to 1993. It was the second qualifying cycle of the World Chess Championship. The participants were selected from the top players of the Zonal tournaments. The top-ranking players qualified for the Candidates Tournament. Since 1998 the winners of the zonal tournaments have played short matches against each other over a few weeks in a knockout-style competition to determine who is eligible for the Candidates Tournament.

### IQP
`wikipedia:glossary:IQP` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

IQP: Abbr. for isolated queen pawn. See isolani.

### irregular opening
`wikipedia:glossary:irregular opening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

irregular opening: Early 19th-century chess literature classified all openings that did not begin with either 1.e4 e5 or 1.d4 d5 as "irregular". As opening theory developed and many openings previously considered "irregular" became standard (e.g. the Sicilian Defense), the term gradually became less common. Opening books today are more likely to describe debuts such as 1.b4 (the Sokolsky Opening) as "uncommon" or "unorthodox".

### isolani
`wikipedia:glossary:isolani` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

isolani: Refers to a d-pawn with no pawns of the same color on the adjacent c-file and e-file, and is a synonym for isolated queen pawn. Aron Nimzowitsch, who coined the term, regarded the isolani as a weapon of attack in the middlegame but an endgame weakness; he saw the problem of hanging pawns as related. See also Pawn structure § Queen's Gambit – Isolani Formation.

### isolated pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:isolated pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

isolated pawn: A pawn with no pawn of the same color on an adjacent file.

### Italian bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:Italian bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Italian bishop: A white bishop developed to c4 or a black bishop developed to c5. A bishop so developed is characteristic of the Italian Game. In the Giuoco Piano both players have Italian bishops. The Italian bishop stands in contrast to the Spanish bishop on b5 characteristic of the Ruy Lopez. "Italian" may be used as an adjective for an opening where one or both players have Italian bishops.

### j'adoube
`wikipedia:glossary:j'adoube` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

j'adoube: [from French, 'I adjust', pronounced [ʒa.dub]] The international signal of the intention to adjust the position of a piece on the board. When playing with the touch-move rule, a player must say this in order to be able to touch a piece without being subject to the touched piece rule. The verb adouber, literally "to dub" (raise to the knighthood), is rarely used in contemporary French outside of this context. A local language equivalent, e.g. "I am adjusting", is generally also acceptable.

### K
`wikipedia:glossary:K` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

K: Symbol used for the king when recording chess moves in English.

### key square
`wikipedia:glossary:key square` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

key square: 1. An important square. 2. In pawn endings, a square whose occupation by one side's king guarantees the achievement of a certain goal, such as the promotion of a pawn or the win of a pawn. Also called critical square.

### kibitz
`wikipedia:glossary:kibitz` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

kibitz: As a spectator, making comments on a chess game that can be heard by the players. Kibitzing on a serious game while it is in progress (rather than during a post-mortem) is a serious breach of chess etiquette.

### kick
`wikipedia:glossary:kick` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

kick: Attacking a piece, often a knight, with a pawn, so that it will move. Kicking a piece may lead to gaining a tempo, or may force the opponent to concede control of key squares.

### king
`wikipedia:glossary:king` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king: The most important piece in chess. It may move to any adjacent square, and it may castle. A king threatened with capture is in check; a player cannot end their move with their king in check. If a player's king is in check and there is no escape, then the king is in checkmate, and the player loses. If the player whose turn it is has no legal moves and their king is not in check, then it is stalemate, and the game is drawn.

### king bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:king bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king bishop: Or king's bishop. The bishop that is on the kingside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "KB".

### king hunt
`wikipedia:glossary:king hunt` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king hunt: A sustained attack on the enemy king that results in the king being driven a far distance from its initial position, typically resulting in its checkmate. Some of the most famous games featuring king hunts are Edward Lasker–Thomas, Polugaevsky–Nezhmetdinov, and Kasparov–Topalov. Also called king chase.

### king knight
`wikipedia:glossary:king knight` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king knight: Or king's knight. The knight that is on the kingside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "KN".

### king pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:king pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king pawn: Or king's pawn. A pawn on the king's file, i.e. the e-file. Sometimes abbreviated "KP". Also king bishop pawn (KBP), king knight pawn (KNP), and king rook pawn (KRP) for a pawn on the f-, g-, or h-file, respectively.

### king pawn opening
`wikipedia:glossary:king pawn opening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king pawn opening: Or king's pawn opening. An opening that begins 1.e4.

### king rook
`wikipedia:glossary:king rook` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king rook: Or king's rook. The rook that is on the kingside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "KR".

### kingside
`wikipedia:glossary:kingside` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

kingside: Or king's side. The side of the board (half-board) the kings are on at the start of the game (the e- through h-file), as opposed to the queenside. Also called king's wing.

### king walk
`wikipedia:glossary:king walk` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

king walk: A consecutive series of king moves designed to bring the king to a safer square. For example, if a player has castled kingside but the opponent has sacrificed a piece to destroy the kingside pawn cover, they may choose to walk the king over to the queenside to shelter behind the queenside pawns.

### knight
`wikipedia:glossary:knight` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

knight: A piece that may move to any nearest square not on a rank, file, or diagonal on which it stands. In other words, it may move two squares horizontally or vertically and then one square perpendicular to that (forming an L shape), jumping over any pieces in the way.

### knight pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:knight pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

knight pawn: Or knight's pawn. A pawn on the knight's file, i.e. the b-file or g-file. Sometimes abbreviated "NP".

### knight's tour
`wikipedia:glossary:knight's tour` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

knight's tour: A puzzle that challenges a person to set a knight on an empty chessboard, and make the piece move around (as it moves in a chess game), but to visit every square only once. The knight's tour is the best known of a variety of tours and puzzles based on chess pieces. A closed tour (also known as a re-entrant tour) ends on the same square on which it began and needs 64 moves. An open tour ends on a different square and needs only 63 moves.

### Kotov syndrome
`wikipedia:glossary:Kotov syndrome` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Kotov syndrome: This phenomenon, described by Alexander Kotov in his 1971 book Think Like a Grandmaster, can occur when a player does not find a good plan after thinking long and hard on a position. The player, under time pressure, then suddenly decides to make a move that they have hardly thought about at all, and it may not be a good move for that reason.

### Kriegspiel
`wikipedia:glossary:Kriegspiel` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Kriegspiel: [from German, 'war game'] Kriegspiel is a chess variant played by two opponents who can see only their own board, and one monitoring umpire who makes the moves of both players on a neutral board. It requires three chess sets and boards. The players make their moves based on limited information from the umpire. It was introduced in 1898. It is sometimes referred to as blind chess, not to be confused with blindfold chess.

### Kt
`wikipedia:glossary:Kt` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Kt: The symbol sometimes used for the knight when recording chess moves in descriptive notation, mainly in older literature. An N is used instead in algebraic notation and in later descriptive notation to avoid confusion with K, the symbol for the king.

### lightning chess
`wikipedia:glossary:lightning chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

lightning chess: A form of chess with an extremely short time limit, either blitz chess or bullet chess.

### light-square bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:light-square bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

light-square bishop: Often shortened to light bishop. One of the two bishops that moves only on the light squares. In the starting position, White's light-square bishop is on f1; Black's is on c8. cf. dark-square bishop.

### light squares
`wikipedia:glossary:light squares` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

light squares: The 32 light-colored squares on the chessboard, such as h1 and a8. cf. dark squares.

### line
`wikipedia:glossary:line` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

line: 1. A sequence of moves, usually in the opening or in analyzing a position. 2. An open path for a piece (queen, rook, or bishop) to move or control squares.

### line piece
`wikipedia:glossary:line piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

line piece: A piece whose movement is defined to be along straight lines of squares (i.e. the rook, bishop, and queen).

### long diagonal
`wikipedia:glossary:long diagonal` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

long diagonal: One of the two diagonals with eight squares (a1–h8 or h1–a8).

### long fianchetto
`wikipedia:glossary:long fianchetto` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

long fianchetto: A fianchetto whereby the knight's pawn has advanced two squares (b4 or g4 for White; b5 or g5 for Black) instead of one.

### loose piece
`wikipedia:glossary:loose piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

loose piece: A piece vulnerable to opponent attacks because it is undefended and cannot easily be withdrawn or supported.

### loose position
`wikipedia:glossary:loose position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

loose position: A position vulnerable to opponent attacks because it is overextended or its pieces are uncoordinated.

### loss
`wikipedia:glossary:loss` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

loss: A defeat for one of the two players, which may occur due to that player being checkmated by the other player, resigning, exceeding the time control, or being forfeited by the tournament director. In chess, a zero-sum game, this results in a win for the other player.

### Lucena position
`wikipedia:glossary:Lucena position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Lucena position: A well-known rook and pawn versus rook endgame position in which the player with the extra pawn can force a win by cutting off the opponent's king and placing a rook on the 4th rank in order to block the opponent's rook checks, thereby allowing the pawn to queen.

### luft
`wikipedia:glossary:luft` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

luft: [from German 'air'] Space made for a castled king to give it a flight square to prevent a back-rank mate. Usually luft is made by moving a pawn on the second rank in front of the king. See also flight square.

### main line
`wikipedia:glossary:main line` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

main line: The principal, most important, or most often played variation of an opening.

### majority
`wikipedia:glossary:majority` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

majority: A larger number of pawns on one flank opposed by a smaller number of the opponent's; often a player with a majority on one flank has a minority on the other. A central pawn majority is a larger number of pawns on the center files.

### major piece
`wikipedia:glossary:major piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

major piece: A queen or rook, also known as a heavy piece. The primary distinction of major pieces versus minor pieces is that major pieces are capable of checkmate with only their own king for support, as the enemy king is unable to step across the ranks and files they control. On an otherwise empty board, a major piece can move from any square to any other square in at most two moves.

### man
`wikipedia:glossary:man` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

man: A piece or a pawn, when the term "piece" is used as exclusive of pawns.

### Maróczy Bind
`wikipedia:glossary:Maróczy Bind` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Maróczy Bind: A bind on the light squares in the center, particularly d5, obtained by White by placing pawns on c4 and e4. Named for Géza Maróczy, it originally referred to formations arising in some variations of the Sicilian Defense, but the name is now also applied to similar setups in the English Opening and the Queen's Indian Defense. It was once greatly feared by Black but means of countering it have been developed since the 1980s and earlier.

### master
`wikipedia:glossary:master` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

master: Loosely, a strong chess player who would be expected to beat most amateurs. It may also refer to a formal title such as International Master or National Master. Standards vary, but a master will usually have an Elo rating of over 2200.

### match
`wikipedia:glossary:match` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

match: The term "match" does not refer to an individual game of chess, but to either a competition between two teams or a series of games between two individuals. A match may be the entire competition, or it may be a round in a knockout tournament or team tournament. A match between individuals usually consists of several games, continuing until one of the players has achieved either a set score or a set number of wins.

### material
`wikipedia:glossary:material` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

material: A player's pieces and pawns on the board. The player with pieces and pawns of total greater value is said to have a material advantage. Gaining a material advantage is called winning material. See Chess piece relative value.

### materialism
`wikipedia:glossary:materialism` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

materialism: Playstyle characterized by a willingness to win material at the expense of positional considerations. Chess engines historically were often materialistic.

### mating attack
`wikipedia:glossary:mating attack` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

mating attack: An attack aimed at checkmating the enemy king.

### mating net
`wikipedia:glossary:mating net` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

mating net: A position or series of moves that leads to forced mate.

### MCO
`wikipedia:glossary:MCO` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

MCO: Abbr. for Modern Chess Openings, a popular chess opening reference. Often the edition is also given, as in MCO-14, the 14th edition. cf. ECO.

### middlegame
`wikipedia:glossary:middlegame` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

middlegame: The part of a chess game that follows the opening and comes before the endgame, beginning after the pieces are developed in the opening. This is usually roughly moves 20 through 40.

### miniature
`wikipedia:glossary:miniature` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

miniature: A short game (usually no more than 20 to 25 moves), for example: 1.e3 e5 2.Qf3 d5 3.Nc3 e4 4.Qf4?? Bd6! and White resigned in Spiel–Künzel, Europe 1900, because the queen is trapped. However, some authors include games up to 30 moves. Usually only decisive games (not draws) are considered miniatures. Ideally, a miniature should not be spoiled by an obvious blunder by the losing side. A miniature may also qualify as a brilliancy. The Opera Game is a famous example. Sometimes called a brevity [chiefly British]. See also Glossary of chess problems § miniature.

### minority
`wikipedia:glossary:minority` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

minority: A smaller number of pawns on one flank opposed by a larger number of the opponent's; often a player with a minority on one flank has a majority on the other.

### minority attack
`wikipedia:glossary:minority attack` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

minority attack: An advance of pawns on the side of the board where one has fewer pawns than the opponent, an attack strategy usually carried out to provoke a weakness.

### minor piece
`wikipedia:glossary:minor piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

minor piece: A bishop or knight. Unlike major pieces, minor pieces are unable to contain the enemy king or block his advance alone, as he can simply pass through the holes in their line of attack. Compared to major pieces, minor pieces also find it difficult to navigate the entire board; a knight may require four moves to reach a square two squares away, while a bishop can only ever control half of all squares.

### mobile pawn center
`wikipedia:glossary:mobile pawn center` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

mobile pawn center: Pawns on central squares able to advance without becoming weak.

### mobility
`wikipedia:glossary:mobility` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

mobility: The ability of a piece(s) to move around the board. Having space.

### move
`wikipedia:glossary:move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

move: 1. A turn taken by one of the players, moving a piece to a new square (sometimes with the capture of an opponent's piece or the promotion of a pawn) or castling. 2. A turn by both players, White and Black: for example, in the context of the 50-move rule, or when referring to the duration or stage of a game (as in "the position after move 15" or "the game ended after 27 moves"). A turn by either White or Black may then be called a half-move, or (in computer contexts) one ply.

### move order
`wikipedia:glossary:move order` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

move order: The sequence of moves one chooses to play an opening or execute a plan. Different move orders often have different advantages and disadvantages. A plan that uses certain moves can sometimes be improved by making the identical moves but in a different sequence. See also transposition.

### mysterious rook move
`wikipedia:glossary:mysterious rook move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

mysterious rook move: Coined by Nimzowitsch to refer to the placing of a rook on a closed file in anticipation that the opponent is going to open the file. This move may either achieve a position with a rook on an open file, or it may alternatively hinder the opponent's intentions (prophylaxis). The meaning of the word has since expanded to refer to any rook move that appears to have a hidden purpose.

### N
`wikipedia:glossary:N` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

N: 1. Symbol used for the knight when recording chess moves in English. 2. Abbr. for novelty.

### NCO
`wikipedia:glossary:NCO` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

NCO: Abbr. for Nunn's Chess Openings, a chess openings reference. cf. ECO and MCO.

### NN
`wikipedia:glossary:NN` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

NN: Traditionally used in game scores to indicate a player whose name is not known, or whose name the editor chooses not to publish (usually the loser). The origin is uncertain. It may be an abbreviation of the Latin nomina ("names"), or it may be short for the Latin phrase nomen nescio ("name unknown"). Sometimes N.N.

### norm
`wikipedia:glossary:norm` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

norm: A step toward earning a chess title, such as Grandmaster or International Master. To qualify for the award of norms, a tournament must be rated by FIDE, must be sufficiently strong, must include a mix of nationalities, must include a specified number of titled players, and must meet certain other requirements regarding time control and playing conditions. The score necessary to qualify for a norm depends on the strength of the tournament. In practice, three norms are usually required for a title, though regulations have varied over the years.

### notation
`wikipedia:glossary:notation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

notation: Any method of recording chess moves, allowing games to be later published, replayed, and analyzed. The most common notation today is algebraic notation, which is used internationally. Formerly descriptive notation was standard in English language publications. There are also systems of notation for recording chess positions without the use of diagrams, the most common of which is Forsyth–Edwards Notation (FEN). cf. annotation.

### occupation
`wikipedia:glossary:occupation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

occupation: Occupation of a rank or file means a rook or queen controls it; occupation of a square means a piece or pawn sits on it.

### octopus
`wikipedia:glossary:octopus` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

octopus: A strongly positioned knight in enemy territory. A knight not near the edge reaches out in eight directions, like the eight tentacles of an octopus.

### odds
`wikipedia:glossary:odds` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

odds: This refers to the stronger player giving the weaker player some sort of advantage in order to make the game more competitive. It may be an advantage in material, in extra moves, in time on the clock, or some combination of those elements. Since the advent of the chess clock, time odds have become more common than material odds.

### Olympiad
`wikipedia:glossary:Olympiad` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Olympiad: An international team chess tournament organized biennially by FIDE. Each team represents a FIDE member country.

### open file
`wikipedia:glossary:open file` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

open file: A file on which there are no pawns. cf. half-open file.

### open game
`wikipedia:glossary:open game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

open game: Or open position. A game in which exchanges have opened files and diagonals, and there are few pawns in the center, as opposed to a closed game. See also positional play.

### Open Game
`wikipedia:glossary:Open Game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Open Game: Any opening that begins with the moves 1.e4 e5. Examples of Open Games include the Ruy Lopez, the Giuoco Piano, the Danish Gambit, and many others. The Open Game is also referred to as a Double King's Pawn Opening or Double King's Pawn Game. cf. Closed Game. See also Semi-Open Game.

### opening
`wikipedia:glossary:opening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opening: The beginning phase of the game, roughly the first dozen moves, but it can extend much farther. In the opening players set up their pawn structures, develop their pieces, and usually castle. The opening precedes the middlegame.

### opening preparation
`wikipedia:glossary:opening preparation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opening preparation: Home study and analysis of openings and defenses that one expects to play, or meet, in later tournament or match games. In high-level play, an important part of this is the search for theoretical novelties that improve upon previous play or previously published analysis.

### opening repertoire
`wikipedia:glossary:opening repertoire` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opening repertoire: The set of openings played by a particular player. The breadth of different players' repertoires varies from very narrow to very broad.

### opening system
`wikipedia:glossary:opening system` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opening system: An opening that is defined by a series of moves by one player, where the moves can be made independently (to some extent) of the moves chosen by the opponent, and are usually not required to be made in a fixed move order, with the goal of reaching a certain type of middlegame position. One example is the Colle System, characterized by an early e3 from White, intending to place the queen's bishop on b2; the London System is another frequently played opening system. Openings for Black can also be described as opening systems, such as the King's Indian Defense.

### open lines
`wikipedia:glossary:open lines` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

open lines: 1. n. Unobstructed files and diagonals. See also open game. 2. v. To move or exchange pawns to bring about unobstructed files and diagonals.

### open tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:open tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

open tournament: A tournament where anyone can enter, regardless of rating or invitation. cf. closed tournament.

### opposite castling
`wikipedia:glossary:opposite castling` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opposite castling: Or opposite-side castling. Describes when one player has castled kingside and the opponent has castled queenside.

### opposition
`wikipedia:glossary:opposition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

opposition: A position in which two kings stand on the same rank, file, or diagonal with one empty square between them. The player to move may be forced to move the king to a less advantageous square. Opposition is a particularly important concept in endgames. One orthogonal square separation is direct opposition; one diagonal square is diagonal opposition; multiple squares separation is distant opposition. cf. corresponding squares.

### outpost
`wikipedia:glossary:outpost` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

outpost: An outpost is a square protected by a pawn that is in or near the enemy's stronghold. Outposts are a favorable position from which one can launch an attack, particularly using a knight.

### outside passed pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:outside passed pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

outside passed pawn: A passed pawn near the edge of the board and not in the path of threats from the opponent's pawns. In the endgame, such a pawn can constitute a strong advantage, because it threatens to promote, and it also diverts the opponent's forces to restrain its advance.

### overextended
`wikipedia:glossary:overextended` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

overextended: An overextended position results when a player has advanced pawns too far into the opponent's side without sufficient support. The premature advance can leave weaknesses in the player's camp or the advanced pawns themselves may be weak ("overextended pawns").

### overloaded
`wikipedia:glossary:overloaded` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

overloaded: A piece that has too many defensive duties. An overloaded piece can sometimes be deflected, or required to abandon one of its defensive duties.

### overprotection
`wikipedia:glossary:overprotection` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

overprotection: The strategy of protecting an important pawn or square more than is apparently necessary. This serves to dissuade the opponent from attacking that point, and the latent power of the "over protectors" assembled around an important point is a significant threat that can bear fruit at a small tactical change in the position. Aron Nimzowitsch coined the term and was a proponent of overprotection.

### over the board ("OTB")
`wikipedia:glossary:over the board ("OTB")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

over the board ("OTB"): 1. An over-the-board game is played face to face with the opponent, as opposed to a remote opponent as in online chess or correspondence chess. 2. Analysis carried out during a game in real time (not necessarily a face-to-face game) as opposed to during preparation. Finding accurate moves over the board is harder than finding them with computer assistance in one's own time. "I looked up the gambit Smith played and there's a line that refutes it, but I couldn't find it over the board."

### P
`wikipedia:glossary:P` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

P: Symbol used for the pawn when recording chess positions in English; a lowercase p is typically used for a Black pawn. Also used for the pawn when recording chess moves in descriptive notation, e.g. P-K4.

### pairing
`wikipedia:glossary:pairing` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pairing: The assignment of opponents in a tournament. The most common pairing methods used in chess tournaments are round-robin and the Swiss system.

### passar battaglia
`wikipedia:glossary:passar battaglia` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

passar battaglia: [from Italian, 'to dodge the fight'] The former rule that a pawn could evade capture by an opposing pawn by its initial two-square advance, in contrast to the en passant rule. Passar battaglia remained the practice in several parts of Europe long after en passant was introduced, and it was not completely abandoned until 1880 when Italy adopted the en passant rule.

### passed pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:passed pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

passed pawn: A pawn that has no pawn of the opposite color on its file or on an adjacent file to challenge or threaten its potential for queening.

### passive
`wikipedia:glossary:passive` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

passive: Describes a piece or pawn that is inactive and able to move to or control relatively few squares, or a position without possibilities for attack or counterplay. Antonym: active.

### passive sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:passive sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

passive sacrifice: The sacrifice of a piece, by moving a different piece, leaving the sacrificed piece under attack.

### pattern recognition
`wikipedia:glossary:pattern recognition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pattern recognition: A part of chess thinking that involves remembering and recognizing certain recurring positional aspects large and small, visual and dynamic. It is a kind of thinking that gives an advantage to a player with great experience. It is distinct from the intellectual activity of calculation. It uses intuitive thinking that is familiar to humans, but is foreign to computers. It can be developed by studying chess puzzles. It has been studied by Adriaan de Groot, and other scientists, who have attempted to discover how chess players think.

### patzer
`wikipedia:glossary:patzer` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

patzer: [from German: patzen, 'to bungle'] A weak chess player. See also woodpusher.

### pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn: A piece that can move one square directly forward, or on its first move, can move two squares directly forward. It can also move one square diagonally forward when capturing. It may capture en passant. Upon reaching its eighth rank, it is promoted to a same-colored queen, rook, bishop, or knight.

### pawn and move
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn and move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn and move: A type of odds game, common in the 18th and 19th centuries, in which the superior player plays Black and begins the game with one of their pawns, usually the king bishop pawn, removed from the board; plus White gets an extra move at the start.

### pawn break
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn break` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn break: A pawn move that attacks an enemy pawn in order to open up lines or challenge the opponent's pawn structure. See also break.

### pawn center
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn center` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn center: Or pawn centre. A player's pawns in the center of the board. Pawns on the squares adjacent to the center may also be considered part of the pawn center. Having a strong pawn center was considered absolutely essential until the hypermodernist school introduced some new ideas. Often shortened to center. See King's Indian Defense, Four Pawns Attack for an example of an opening leading to an extended pawn center.

### pawn chain
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn chain` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn chain: Two or more pawns of the same color diagonally linked. A pawn chain's weakest point is the base because it is not protected by another pawn. See also pawn structure.

### pawn island
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn island` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn island: A group of pawns of one color on consecutive files with no other pawns of the same color on an adjacent file. A pawn island consisting of one pawn is an isolated pawn.

### pawn race
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn race` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn race: A situation where both opponents are pushing a passed pawn in effort to be first to promote.

### pawn roller
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn roller` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn roller: Two connected passed pawns. "Roller" refers to their ability to defend one another as they advance toward promotion.

### pawn storm
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn storm` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn storm: An attacking technique where a group of pawns on one wing is advanced to break up the defense.

### pawn structure
`wikipedia:glossary:pawn structure` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pawn structure: The placement of the pawns during the course of a game. As pawns are the least mobile of the pieces and the only pieces unable to move backwards, the position of the pawns greatly influences the character of the game. Also called pawn skeleton.

### PCA
`wikipedia:glossary:PCA` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

PCA: Abbr. for Professional Chess Association.

### PCO
`wikipedia:glossary:PCO` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

PCO: Abbr. for Practical Chess Openings, the 1948 openings reference book by Reuben Fine. In 2025, another openings reference book of the same name, written by Martyn Kravtsiv, was published. cf. ECO and MCO.

### performance rating ("PR")
`wikipedia:glossary:performance rating ("PR")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

performance rating ("PR"): A number reflecting the approximate rating level at which a player performed in a particular tournament or match. It is often calculated by adding together the player's performances in each individual game, using the opponent's rating for a draw, adding 400 points to the opponent's rating for a win, and subtracting 400 points from the opponent's rating for a loss, then dividing by the total number of games. For example, a player who beat a 2400-rated player, lost to a 2600, drew a 2500, and beat a 2300, would have a performance rating of 2550 (i.e. 2800 + 2200 + 2500 + 2700, divided by 4).

### perpetual check
`wikipedia:glossary:perpetual check` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

perpetual check: Often shortened to perpetual. When a player puts the opponent in check and the check could be repeated endlessly, the game will be declared a draw by repetition. This tactic can be resorted to as a form of insurance in a losing position.

### Philidor position
`wikipedia:glossary:Philidor position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Philidor position: Usually refers to an important chess endgame that illustrates a drawing technique when the defender has a king and rook versus a king, rook, and pawn. It is also known as the third rank defense, because of the importance of the rook on the third rank cutting off the opposing king. It was analyzed by Philidor in 1777. See also Rook and pawn versus rook endgame.

### Philidor sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:Philidor sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Philidor sacrifice: The sacrifice of a minor piece for one or two pawns for greater pawn mobility as compensation.

### piece
`wikipedia:glossary:piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

piece: 1. One of the chessmen or figures used to play the game – that is, a king, queen, rook, bishop, knight or pawn. Each piece type has its own rules of movement on the board and of capturing enemy pieces. This is the definition used in the context of rules of chess – for example, the touched piece rule. 2. When annotating or discussing chess games, the term "piece" usually excludes pawns. It may be used collectively for all "non-pawns" – for example, "White's pieces are well-posted." In some contexts, it may refer specifically to a minor piece – for example, "White is up two pieces for a rook."

### pin
`wikipedia:glossary:pin` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pin: When a piece is attacked but cannot legally move, because doing so would expose the player's own king to the attack ("absolute pin"); or when a piece is attacked and can legally move out of the line of attack, but such a move would expose a more valuable piece (or an unprotected piece) to capture ("relative pin").

### playable
`wikipedia:glossary:playable` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

playable: Said of an opening, a position, or move that affords the person playing it a tenable position.

### play by hand
`wikipedia:glossary:play by hand` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

play by hand: To make a move intuitively and without analyzing the move.

### ply
`wikipedia:glossary:ply` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

ply: Term mainly used in computer chess to denote one play of either White or Black. Thus equal to half a move.

### poisoned pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:poisoned pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

poisoned pawn: An unprotected pawn that, if captured, causes positional problems or material loss.

### Poisoned Pawn Variation
