`wikipedia:glossary:Poisoned Pawn Variation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Poisoned Pawn Variation: Any of several opening varitions, the best-known of these being in the Najdorf Variation of the Sicilian Defense, in which there is a poisoned pawn.

### Portable Game Notation ("PGN")
`wikipedia:glossary:Portable Game Notation ("PGN")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Portable Game Notation ("PGN"): This is a popular computer-processible ASCII format for recording chess games (both the moves and related data). There are import and export versions: the import version is lax, while the export version is not.

### position
`wikipedia:glossary:position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

position: "The disposition of pieces and pawns, of one or both colours, at any stage of the game or as set in a composition." If one side has an overall advantage in strength, that side is said to have "the better position". If neither side has an overall advantage, the position might be called level or equal or balanced. The position of chessmen at the beginning of a game is called an array.

### positional play
`wikipedia:glossary:positional play` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

positional play: Play based on strategy, on gaining and exploiting small advantages, and on analyzing the larger position, rather than calculating the more immediate tactics. cf. antipositional.

### positional player
`wikipedia:glossary:positional player` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

positional player: A player who specializes in positional play, as distinguished from a tactician.

### positional sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:positional sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

positional sacrifice: A sacrifice in which the lost material is not regained via a combination, but instead gains positional compensation. These typically require deep positional understanding and are often overlooked by computers. Also known as a true sacrifice, as opposed to a pseudo sacrifice or sham sacrifice.

### post mortem
`wikipedia:glossary:post mortem` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

post mortem: Analysis of a game after it has concluded, typically by one or both players and sometimes with spectators (kibitzers) contributing as well. A player who has just lost the game thanks to a dubious move has the chance to "win the post-mortem" by finding a better one.

### premove
`wikipedia:glossary:premove` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

premove: In online chess, a move input that is made during the opponent's turn, to take effect only after the opponent has moved. Premoving, the act of making premoves, is a popular way of saving time in blitz and bullet formats.

### prepared variation
`wikipedia:glossary:prepared variation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

prepared variation: A well-analyzed novelty in the opening that is not published but first used against an opponent in competitive play.

### Principle of two weaknesses
`wikipedia:glossary:Principle of two weaknesses` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Principle of two weaknesses: A technique of increasing one's advantage by causing the opponent, who has one weakness, to have a second weakness. Even if both weaknesses are minor, the fact of having two, in practice, becomes a major weakness.

### priyome
`wikipedia:glossary:priyome` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

priyome: A Russian term for particular tactics that depend on pawn structure.

### problem-like
`wikipedia:glossary:problem-like` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

problem-like: An elegant and counterintuitive tactical shot, of the type generally found in chess problems rather than in actual play, can be termed problem-like.

### promotion
`wikipedia:glossary:promotion` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

promotion: Advancing a pawn to the eighth rank, converting it to a queen, rook, bishop, or knight. Promotion to a piece other than a queen is called underpromotion.

### prophylaxis
`wikipedia:glossary:prophylaxis` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

prophylaxis: A strategy that frustrates and protects against an opponent's plan or tactic for fear of the consequences. See also blockade, overprotection, and mysterious rook move.

### protected passed pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:protected passed pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

protected passed pawn: A passed pawn that is supported by another pawn.

### protection
`wikipedia:glossary:protection` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

protection: A piece is protected when another friendly piece controls its square. This somewhat protects the first piece from capture, as there is the option to recapture. This is especially effective if the attacking piece is of greater value than the threatened piece.

### pruning
`wikipedia:glossary:pruning` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

pruning: Chess engines usually include an algorithm to eliminate certain moves from consideration, assuming that they are blunders and therefore irrelevant to the final move selection. Forward pruning runs the risk of overlooking the best move; this risk may be slight to moderate depending on how aggressively the pruning algorithm is set. Alpha–beta pruning is considered a form of backward pruning, which does not risk overlooking the best move.

### push
`wikipedia:glossary:push` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

push: 1. v. To move a pawn forward. 2. n. A pawn move forward.

### Q
`wikipedia:glossary:Q` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Q: Symbol used for the queen when recording chess moves in English.

### quad
`wikipedia:glossary:quad` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

quad: A round-robin style tournament between four players, where each participant plays every other participant once.

### queen
`wikipedia:glossary:queen` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen: 1. n. A piece that may move along ranks, files, and diagonals without jumping. 2. v. To promote a pawn.

### queen bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:queen bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen bishop: Or queen's bishop. The bishop that is on the queenside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "QB".

### queening
`wikipedia:glossary:queening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queening: Promotion to a queen. Also called promoting. Rarely used to indicate promotion to a knight, rook, or bishop (i.e. underpromotion) as well.

### queen knight
`wikipedia:glossary:queen knight` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen knight: Or queen's knight. The knight that is on the queenside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "QN".

### queen pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:queen pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen pawn: Or queen's pawn. A pawn on the queen's file, i.e. the d-file. Sometimes abbreviated "QP". Also queen rook pawn (QRP), queen knight pawn (QNP), and queen bishop pawn (QBP) for a pawn on the a-, b-, or c-file, respectively.

### queen pawn opening
`wikipedia:glossary:queen pawn opening` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen pawn opening: Or queen's pawn opening. An opening that begins 1.d4.

### queen rook
`wikipedia:glossary:queen rook` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queen rook: Or queen's rook. The rook that is on the queenside at the start of the game. Sometimes abbreviated "QR".

### queenside
`wikipedia:glossary:queenside` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

queenside: Or queen's side. The side of the board (board-half) the queens are on at the start of the game (the a- through d-file), as opposed to the kingside. Also called queen's wing.

### question
`wikipedia:glossary:question` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

question: Usually in the form "put the question to [a piece]", to force the opponent to commit to a strategic decision regarding the future of a piece. Most commonly used to refer to a one-square advance of a rook's pawn to attack a bishop that is pinning or pressuring a knight, forcing the opponent to decide whether to retreat the bishop or exchange it for the knight. "Putting the question to the bishop" may resolve the pin, but can also weaken the player's pawn structure. This usage originates in Aron Nimzowitsch's My System (1925).

### quiet move
`wikipedia:glossary:quiet move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

quiet move: A move that does not attack or capture an enemy piece.

### R
`wikipedia:glossary:R` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

R: Symbol used for the rook when recording chess moves in English.

### Rabar Classification
`wikipedia:glossary:Rabar Classification` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Rabar Classification: A system of opening classification codes introduced by Braslav Rabar for Chess Informant. The system was used by Informant publications from 1966 to 1981 but has since been replaced by ECO codes.

### randomized chess
`wikipedia:glossary:randomized chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

randomized chess: "A form of unorthodox chess designed to discount knowledge of the openings. The pawns are placed as in the array and behind them the pieces are placed in unorthodox fashion." See also Fischerandom.

### rank
`wikipedia:glossary:rank` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

rank: A row of squares of the chessboard. In algebraic notation, ranks are numbered 1–8 starting from White's side of the board; however, players customarily refer to ranks from their own perspectives. For example: White's king and other pieces start on their first (or "back" or "home") rank, whereas Black calls the same rank the "eighth" (or last) rank; White's seventh rank is Black's second; and so on. If neither perspective is given, White's view is assumed. This relative reference to ranks was formalized in the older descriptive notation.

### rapid chess
`wikipedia:glossary:rapid chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

rapid chess: A form of chess with reduced time limit, usually 30 minutes per player. Also called active chess and action chess.

### recapture
`wikipedia:glossary:recapture` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

recapture: The capture of an opponent's piece that previously made a capture, and usually played immediately following the opponent's capture move. The capture and recapture occur on the same square, and usually the pieces captured and recaptured have the same value.

### refute
`wikipedia:glossary:refute` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

refute: To demonstrate that a strategy, move, or opening is not as good as previously thought (often, that it leads to a loss), or that previously published analysis is unsound. A refutation is sometimes colloquially referred to as a bust. A refutation in the context of chess problems or endgame studies is often called a cook.

### relative pin
`wikipedia:glossary:relative pin` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

relative pin: A pin where it is legal to move the pinned piece out of the line of attack. The piece is not pinned therefore to the king, but instead to some other piece. Contrast with absolute pin where the pinned piece is not permitted to move, because the piece it is pinned to is the king.

### remis
`wikipedia:glossary:remis` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

remis: [from French] A draw. It literally means 'reset' and is somewhat archaic (the usual word for a draw in modern French is nulle), but is internationally understood and may be used between players without a common language.

### reply
`wikipedia:glossary:reply` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

reply: Any move by Black after a move by White, or vice versa.

### reserve tempo
`wikipedia:glossary:reserve tempo` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

reserve tempo: A move a player has available. Such a move may not be crucial to the position on the board, but being able to force the opponent to move by making a reserve move can on occasion result in a significant advantage.

### resign
`wikipedia:glossary:resign` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

resign: To concede loss of the game. A resignation is usually indicated by stopping the clocks, sometimes by offering a handshake, or by saying "I resign". A traditional way to resign is by tipping over one's king. It is common for a game to be resigned, rather than for it to end with checkmate, because experienced players can foresee the checkmate. However, under FIDE Laws, a player's resignation results in a draw if there is no sequence of legal moves that could lead to their opponent checkmating them.

### resign on time
`wikipedia:glossary:resign on time` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

resign on time: A player who intentionally runs out of time to avoid having to resign in a hopeless position can be said to have resigned on time. This is usually performed in a more subtle manner than that of Curt von Bardeleben walking out of the tournament hall against Wilhelm Steinitz. A player low on time and in a losing position may simply "forget" to pay any attention to the clock. Resigning on time when a considerable amount of time was left beforehand is called "stalling" and is considered bad sportsmanship.

### roll
`wikipedia:glossary:roll` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

roll: To move a passed pawn forward, especially to promotion. See also pawn roller.

### Romantic chess
`wikipedia:glossary:Romantic chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Romantic chess: Romantic chess was the style of chess prevalent in the 19th century. It is characterized by bold attacks and sacrifices.

### rook
`wikipedia:glossary:rook` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

rook: A piece that may move along ranks and files without jumping.

### rook lift
`wikipedia:glossary:rook lift` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

rook lift: A maneuver that places a rook in front of its own pawns, often on the third or fourth rank. This can allow the rook to treat a half-open file as if it were an open file, or a closed file as if it were half-open.

### rook pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:rook pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

rook pawn: Or rook's pawn. A pawn on the rook's file, i.e. the a-file or h-file. Sometimes abbreviated "RP".

### round-robin tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:round-robin tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

round-robin tournament: This is a tournament in which each participant plays every other participant an equal number of times. In a double round-robin tournament the participants play each other exactly twice, once with white and once with black. A round robin tournament is commonly used if the number of participants is relatively small. See also Swiss tournament.

### royal fork
`wikipedia:glossary:royal fork` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

royal fork: A fork threatening the king and queen, typically performed with a knight.

### royal piece
`wikipedia:glossary:royal piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

royal piece: A king or queen. In chess variants, the term refers to any piece that must be protected from capture; under this definition, only the king is royal in orthodox chess.

### S
`wikipedia:glossary:S` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

S: [from German: Springer 'jumper'] Alternative notation for the knight. Used rather than K, which means king.

### sac
`wikipedia:glossary:sac` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sac: Short for sacrifice, usually used to describe a sacrifice for a mating attack.

### sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sacrifice: A move or capture that voluntarily gives up material in return for an advantage such as space, development, or an attack. A sacrifice in the opening is called a gambit, especially when applied to a pawn.

### SAN
`wikipedia:glossary:SAN` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

SAN: Abbr. for standard algebraic notation or short algebraic notation (e.g. 1.Nf3), as opposed to long algebraic notation (e.g. 1.Ng1-f3).

### scalp
`wikipedia:glossary:scalp` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

scalp: [slang] To defeat a much higher-ranked player, especially a titled player.

### Scholar's mate
`wikipedia:glossary:Scholar's mate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Scholar's mate: A four-move checkmate (common among novices) in which White plays 1.e4, follows with Qh5 (or Qf3) and Bc4, and finishes with 4.Qxf7#.

### score
`wikipedia:glossary:score` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

score: 1. The recorded moves in a game. See game score. 2. A player's score in a match or tournament, which is almost always 1 point for each win and ½ point for each draw. See Chess scoring.

### score sheet
`wikipedia:glossary:score sheet` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

score sheet: The sheet of paper used to record a game in progress. During formal games, it is usual for both players to record the game using a score sheet. A completed score sheet contains the game score.

### sealed move
`wikipedia:glossary:sealed move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sealed move: To prevent unfair advantage when an OTB game is adjourned, the player whose turn it is to move is required to write down their next move and put it in a sealed envelope. Upon resumption, the arbiter opens the sealed envelope, makes the move and the game continues. The player may be disqualified if the sealed move is illegal, ambiguous or unclear. Adjournments and sealed moves are no longer standard practice. See also Adjournment (games).

### second
`wikipedia:glossary:second` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

second: An assistant hired to help a player in preparation for and during a major match or tournament. The second assists in areas such as opening preparation. The second also used to assist with adjournment analysis before the practice of adjournments was largely abandoned in the 1990s.

### second player
`wikipedia:glossary:second player` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

second player: The expression "the second player" is sometimes used to refer to Black.

### Semi-Closed Game
`wikipedia:glossary:Semi-Closed Game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Semi-Closed Game: An opening that begins with White playing 1.d4 and Black replying with a move other than 1...d5. Also called half-closed game. See also Closed Game.

### Semi-Open Game
`wikipedia:glossary:Semi-Open Game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Semi-Open Game: An opening that begins with White playing 1.e4 and Black replying with a move other than 1...e5. Also called half-open game. See also Open Game.

### seventy-five-move rule
`wikipedia:glossary:seventy-five-move rule` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

seventy-five-move rule: The game is drawn if no capture or pawn move has occurred in the last seventy-five moves by either side. The fifty-move rule operates on a similar principle but is invoked at a player's discretion instead of automatically.

### sham sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:sham sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sham sacrifice: An offer of material that is made at no risk, as acceptance would lead to the gain of equal or greater material or checkmate. This is in contrast to a true sacrifice in which the compensation is less tangible. Also called pseudo sacrifice.

### sharp
`wikipedia:glossary:sharp` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sharp: Highly tactical, risky, double-edged, few available accurate moves. Sharp can be used to describe moves, maneuvers, positions, opening lines, and styles of play.

### shot
`wikipedia:glossary:shot` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

shot: [colloq.] An unexpected or sharp move that typically makes a tactical threat or technical challenge for the opponent.

### silent move
`wikipedia:glossary:silent move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

silent move: A move that has a dynamic tactical effect on a position, but that does not capture or attack an enemy piece. See also quiet move.

### simplification
`wikipedia:glossary:simplification` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

simplification: A strategy of exchanging pieces, often with one of the following goals: as a defensive measure to reduce the size of an attacking force; when having the advantage to reduce the opponent's counterplay; to try to obtain a draw; or as an attempt to gain an advantage by players who are strong in endgame play with simplified positions. Also called liquidation.

### simultaneous chess
`wikipedia:glossary:simultaneous chess` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

simultaneous chess: A form of chess in which one player plays against several players simultaneously. It is usually an exhibition.

### sitzfleisch
`wikipedia:glossary:sitzfleisch` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sitzfleisch: [from German, 'sitting flesh'] The ability to sit still.

### skewer
`wikipedia:glossary:skewer` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

skewer: An attack on a valuable piece, compelling it to move to avoid capture and thus expose a less valuable piece which can then be taken. See also X-ray.

### skittles
`wikipedia:glossary:skittles` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

skittles: A casual or "pickup" game, usually played without a chess clock. At chess tournaments, a skittles room is where one goes to play for fun while waiting for the next formal game.

### slow
`wikipedia:glossary:slow` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

slow: Describes a strategy that requires too many tempi to complete, allowing the opponent time to consolidate.

### smothered mate
`wikipedia:glossary:smothered mate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

smothered mate: A checkmate delivered by a knight in which the mated king is unable to escape because it is surrounded (or "smothered") by its own pieces.

### Sofia rules
`wikipedia:glossary:Sofia rules` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Sofia rules: In the tournament played by Sofia rules, players are not allowed to draw by agreement. They could have draws by stalemate, threefold repetition, fifty-move rule, or insufficient material. Other draws are allowed only if the arbiter declares the game reached a drawn position.

### solid
`wikipedia:glossary:solid` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

solid: An adjective used to describe a move, opening, or manner of play that is characterized by minimal risk-taking and emphasis on quiet positional play rather than wild tactics.

### sortie
`wikipedia:glossary:sortie` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sortie: A queen development in front of its own pawns, often early in the opening, usually for the purpose of exploiting an advantage in space or punishing an error by the opponent. So called because the queen is usually developed behind its own pawns for its protection.

### sound
`wikipedia:glossary:sound` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sound: A correct move or plan. A sound sacrifice has sufficient compensation, a sound opening or variation has no known refutation, and a sound puzzle or composition has no known cooks. Antonym: unsound.

### space
`wikipedia:glossary:space` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

space: The squares controlled by each player. A player controlling more squares than the other is said to have a spatial advantage.

### Spanish bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:Spanish bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Spanish bishop: A white king bishop developed to b5. This is characteristic of the Ruy Lopez, also known as the Spanish Opening.

### spite check
`wikipedia:glossary:spite check` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

spite check: A harmless check given by a player who is about to lose the game, that serves no purpose other than to momentarily delay the defeat.

### squeeze
`wikipedia:glossary:squeeze` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

squeeze: Making pawn moves that limit mobility, freedom and options for the opponent, typically causing a zugzwang.

### staircase maneuver
`wikipedia:glossary:staircase maneuver` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

staircase maneuver: A tactic by which a queen, rook, or king progresses along a diagonal by making a series of lateral steps using a series of checks or alternating with pins and checks. Also called staircase movement.

### stalemate
`wikipedia:glossary:stalemate` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

stalemate: A position in which the player whose turn it is to move has no legal move and their king is not in check. A stalemate results in an immediate draw.

### starting square
`wikipedia:glossary:starting square` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

starting square: A piece's starting square is the square it occupies at the beginning of the game.

### Staunton chess set
`wikipedia:glossary:Staunton chess set` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Staunton chess set: The standard design of chess pieces, required for use in competition.

### stem game
`wikipedia:glossary:stem game` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

stem game: A stem game is the chess game featuring the first use of a particular opening variation. Sometimes, the player or the venue of the stem game is then used to refer to that opening.

### strategic crush
`wikipedia:glossary:strategic crush` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

strategic crush: Win characterized by gradual accumulation of advantages and complete prevention of counterplay.

### strategy
`wikipedia:glossary:strategy` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

strategy: The basis of a player's moves. The evaluation of positions and ways to achieve goals. Strategy is often contrasted with tactics, which are the calculations of more immediate plans and combinations.

### strong
`wikipedia:glossary:strong` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

strong: An effective and well-placed piece or pawn; a potential outpost; a forceful or good move; a position having good winning chances; a highly rated player or one successful in tournaments; or a tournament having a sizable number of strong players competing, such as grandmasters. A "strong showing" refers to a player's high win ratio in a tournament. Antonym: weak

### stronger side
`wikipedia:glossary:stronger side` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

stronger side: The side with a material or positional advantage.

### strongpoint
`wikipedia:glossary:strongpoint` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

strongpoint: 1. A "strongpoint defense" means an opening that defends and retains a central pawn (White: e4 or d4; Black: e5 or d5), as opposed to exchanging the pawn and relinquishing occupation of that central square. 2. More generically, a strongpoint can be any square heavily defended.

### strong square
`wikipedia:glossary:strong square` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

strong square: A square on a player's 4th or greater rank on which the player can post a piece that cannot or will not be driven away by enemy pawns. cf. weak square.

### sudden death
`wikipedia:glossary:sudden death` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

sudden death: The most straightforward time control for a chess game: each player has a fixed amount of time available to make all moves. See also fast chess.

### support point
`wikipedia:glossary:support point` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

support point: A square that cannot be attacked by a pawn, and that can be occupied as a home base for a piece, usually a knight.

### swindle
`wikipedia:glossary:swindle` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

swindle: A ruse or trick played from a position that is inferior.

### Swiss tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:Swiss tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Swiss tournament: A system used in tournaments to determine pairings. In every round each player is paired with an opponent with the same or similar score. See also round-robin tournament.

### symmetry
`wikipedia:glossary:symmetry` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

symmetry: A symmetrical position on the chessboard means the positions of one's pieces are exactly mirrored by the opponent's pieces. This most often occurs when Black mimics White's opening moves. Black is said to break symmetry when making a move that no longer imitates White's move.

### tabia
`wikipedia:glossary:tabia` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tabia: [from Arabic: طبيعة ṭabīʕa 'essence'] Also tabiya. In chess openings a tabia is a key point. It may be a well-known "point of departure" where variations branch off, it may be a position that is reached so often that the real game begins after this initial series of book moves.

### tactician
`wikipedia:glossary:tactician` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tactician: A player who specializes in tactical play, as distinguished from a positional player.

### tactics
`wikipedia:glossary:tactics` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tactics: Combinations, traps, and threats. Play characterized by short-term attacks, requiring calculation by the players, as distinguished from positional play.

### takeback
`wikipedia:glossary:takeback` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

takeback: Used in casual games whereby both players agree to undo one or more moves.

### tall pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:tall pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tall pawn: [colloq.] An ineffective bishop, usually a bad bishop hemmed in by its own pawns.

### Tarrasch rule
`wikipedia:glossary:Tarrasch rule` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Tarrasch rule: The general principle that rooks usually should be placed behind passed pawns, either one's own or one's opponent's. Named after Siegbert Tarrasch.

### technique
`wikipedia:glossary:technique` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

technique: The manner in which a player converts an advantageous position into a win.

### tempo
`wikipedia:glossary:tempo` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tempo: A unit of time considered as one move. A player may gain a tempo in the opening when the opponent moves the same piece twice. In the endgame, one may wish to lose a tempo by triangulation in order to gain the opposition. pl. tempos or tempi.

### tension
`wikipedia:glossary:tension` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tension: A position in which one or more exchanges are possible, such as a pair of pawns facing each other on a diagonal where either can capture the other, is said to contain tension. Such a situation differs from a threat in that it does not need to be immediately resolved – for example, if both pawns are defended. The consequences of resolving the tension must be constantly considered by both players, in case there is a possibility of winning or losing material. This makes calculating the best move more complicated, and so there is a natural temptation to "release the tension" by making a like-for-like exchange (see simplification) or by moving the attacked piece. To "keep the tension" is to avoid resolving it, which can be good advice depending on the position.

### text move
`wikipedia:glossary:text move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

text move: This term is used in written analysis of chess games to refer to a move actually played in the game as opposed to other possible moves. Often shortened to text, for example "The text is inferior as it allows ...f5." Text moves are usually in bold whereas analysis moves are not.

### thematic
`wikipedia:glossary:thematic` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

thematic: Suited to the demands of the position. The term "thematic move" is often applied to the key move of a thematic plan.

### theme tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:theme tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

theme tournament: A chess tournament in which every game must begin with a particular opening specified by the organizers, for example the Budapest Gambit (1.d4 Nf6 2.c4 e5).

### theoretical novelty ("TN" or "N")
`wikipedia:glossary:theoretical novelty ("TN" or "N")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

theoretical novelty ("TN" or "N"): Or simply novelty. A move in the opening that has not been played before.

### threat
`wikipedia:glossary:threat` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

threat: A plan or move that carries an intention to damage the opponent's position. A threat is a tactical weapon that must be defended against.

### threefold repetition
`wikipedia:glossary:threefold repetition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

threefold repetition: A draw may be claimed if the same position occurs three times with the same player to move and with each player having the choice of the same set of moves each time, including the right to capture en passant and the right to castle. For the same position occurring five times, see fivefold repetition.

### time
`wikipedia:glossary:time` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

time: 1. The amount of time each player has to think and calculate as measured by a chess clock. 2. The number of moves to complete an objective; for example, if a king is racing to stop a pawn from queening, and the king has too few moves, that may be referred to as "not enough time". See also tempo.

### time control
`wikipedia:glossary:time control` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

time control: The allowed time to play a game, usually measured by a chess clock. A time control can require either a certain number of moves be made per time period (e.g. 40 moves in 2+1⁄2 hours) or it can limit the length of the entire game (e.g. five minutes per game for blitz). Hybrid schemes are used, and time delay controls have become popular since the widespread use of digital clocks.

### time delay
`wikipedia:glossary:time delay` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

time delay: A time control that makes it possible for a player to avoid having an ever-decreasing amount of time remaining (as is the case with sudden death). The most important time delays in chess are Bronstein delay and Fischer delay.

### time pressure
`wikipedia:glossary:time pressure` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

time pressure: Or time trouble. Having very little time on one's clock (especially less than five minutes) to complete one's remaining moves. Also called zeitnot. See also time control.

### touch-move rule
`wikipedia:glossary:touch-move rule` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

touch-move rule: Or touched piece rule. The rule that requires a player who touches a piece to move that piece unless the piece has no legal moves. If a player moves a piece to a particular square and takes their hand off it, the move must be to that square if it is a legal move. Castling must be initiated by moving the king first, so a player who touches their rook may be required to move the rook, without castling. The rule also requires a player who touches an opponent's piece to capture it if possible. In order to adjust the position of a piece within its square without being required to move it, the player should say "J'adoube" or "I adjust".

### tournament
`wikipedia:glossary:tournament` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tournament: A competition involving more than two players or teams, generally played at a single venue (or series of venues) in a relatively short period of time. A tournament is divided into rounds, with each round consisting either of individual games or matches in the case of knockout tournaments and team tournaments. The assignment of opponents is called pairing, with the most popular systems being round-robin and Swiss. A tournament is usually referred to by the city in which it was played and the year, such as "London 1851", although there are well-known exceptions, such as "AVRO 1938".

### tournament book
`wikipedia:glossary:tournament book` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tournament book: A book recording the scores of all the games in a tournament, usually with analysis of the best or most important games and some background on the event and its participants. One well-known example is Bronstein's Zurich International Chess Tournament 1953. The less comprehensive tournament bulletin is usually issued between the rounds of a prestigious event, giving the players and world media an instant record of the games of the previous round. Individual copies may be bundled together at the conclusion of the event to provide an inexpensive alternative to the tournament book.

### tournament director ("TD")
`wikipedia:glossary:tournament director ("TD")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tournament director ("TD"): Also tournament controller [chiefly British]. Organizer and arbiter of a tournament, responsible for enforcing the tournament rules and the laws of chess.

### tournament performance rating ("TPR")
`wikipedia:glossary:tournament performance rating ("TPR")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tournament performance rating ("TPR"): The performance rating over the course of a tournament.

### TPR
`wikipedia:glossary:TPR` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

TPR: Abbr. for tournament performance rating.

### transposition
`wikipedia:glossary:transposition` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

transposition: Arriving at a position using a different sequence of moves than usual.

### trap
`wikipedia:glossary:trap` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

trap: A move that may tempt the opponent to play a losing move. See also swindle and List of chess traps.

### trapped piece
`wikipedia:glossary:trapped piece` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

trapped piece: A piece is said to be "trapped" when it is en prise and cannot escape its capture in any way (i.e by capturing a piece, moving the piece, or blocking the attacking piece). Any piece—including the queen—can become "trapped" if a player is careless. A trapped king is said to be checkmated.

### trébuchet
`wikipedia:glossary:trébuchet` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

trébuchet: [from French, a type of siege engine] A theoretical position of mutual zugzwang in which either player would lose if it were their turn to move.

### triangulation
`wikipedia:glossary:triangulation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

triangulation: A technique of moving a king along the vertices of a triangle to renter the same position but inverting the player to move, often used in king and pawn endgames (less commonly seen with other pieces) to lose a tempo and gain the opposition.

### tripled pawns
`wikipedia:glossary:tripled pawns` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

tripled pawns: Three pawns of the same color on the same file; considered a weakness due to their inability to defend each other.

### Troitsky line
`wikipedia:glossary:Troitsky line` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Troitsky line: Also Troitzky line. Endgame analysis by Alexey Troitsky of two knights versus a pawn found certain pawn positions that result in win, draw or loss. The resulting pawn positions on each file form what is known as the Troitsky line or Troitsky position.

### two bishops
`wikipedia:glossary:two bishops` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

two bishops: Or the two bishops. A synonym for bishop pair.

### unclear
`wikipedia:glossary:unclear` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

unclear: A position where it is unclear who (if anyone) has an advantage.

### undermining
`wikipedia:glossary:undermining` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

undermining: A tactic (also known as "removal of the guard") in which a defensive piece is captured, leaving one of the opponent's pieces undefended or underdefended.

### underpromotion
`wikipedia:glossary:underpromotion` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

underpromotion: Promoting a pawn to a rook, bishop, or knight instead of a queen. Rarely seen unless the knight can deliver a crucial check, or when promotion to a rook or a bishop instead of a queen is necessary to avoid stalemate.

### United States Chess Federation ("USCF")
`wikipedia:glossary:United States Chess Federation ("USCF")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

United States Chess Federation ("USCF"): This is a nonprofit organization, the governing chess organization within the United States, and one of the federations of the FIDE.

### unpinning
`wikipedia:glossary:unpinning` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

unpinning: The act of breaking a pin by interposing a second piece between the attacker and the target. This allows the piece that was formerly pinned to move.

### USCF
`wikipedia:glossary:USCF` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

USCF: Abbr. for United States Chess Federation.

### vacating sacrifice
`wikipedia:glossary:vacating sacrifice` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

vacating sacrifice: A sacrifice made for the purpose of clearing a square for a different piece of the same color.

### valve
`wikipedia:glossary:valve` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

valve: A move that opens one line and closes another.

### vanished center
`wikipedia:glossary:vanished center` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

vanished center: Or vanished centre. A position with no white or black center pawns.

### variation
`wikipedia:glossary:variation` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

variation: 1. A sequence of moves or an alternative line of play, often applied to the opening. A variation does not have to have been played in a game; it may also be a possibility that occurs only in analysis. Also called continuation. 2. The word "Variation" is also used to name specific sequences of moves within an opening. For an example, the Dragon Variation is part of the Sicilian Defense.

### waiting move
`wikipedia:glossary:waiting move` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

waiting move: A move whose sole purpose is to oblige the opponent to move. A waiting move is effective when the opponent has nothing but bad moves available (i.e. is in zugzwang).

### weakness
`wikipedia:glossary:weakness` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

weakness: A pawn or square that can be attacked and is hard to defend.

### weak square
`wikipedia:glossary:weak square` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

weak square: A square that cannot be easily defended from attack by an opponent. Often a weak square is unable to be defended by pawns (a hole) and can be theoretically occupied by a piece. Exchange or loss of a bishop may make all squares of that bishop's color weak resulting in a "weak square complex" on the light squares or the dark squares.

### white
`wikipedia:glossary:white` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

white: The light-colored squares on the chessboard are often referred to as "the white squares" even though they often are some other light color. Similarly, "the white pieces" are sometimes actually some other (usually light) color. See also black.

### White
`wikipedia:glossary:White` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

White: The designation for the player who moves first, even though the corresponding pieces, referred to as "the white pieces", are sometimes actually some other (usually light) color. See also Black and First-move advantage in chess.

### win
`wikipedia:glossary:win` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

win: A victory for one of the two players in a game, which may occur due to checkmate, resignation by the other player, the other player exceeding the time control, or the other player being forfeited by the tournament director. Chess being a zero-sum game, this results in a loss for the other player. In a tournament a bye may be scored as a win. See also winning position.

### windmill
`wikipedia:glossary:windmill` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

windmill: A combination in which two pieces work together to deliver an alternating series of checks and discovered checks in such a way that the opposing king is required to move on each turn. It is a potent technique, since, on every other move, the discovered check may allow the non-checking piece to capture an enemy piece without losing a tempo. The most famous example is Torre–Lasker, Moscow 1925. Also called seesaw.

### wing
`wikipedia:glossary:wing` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

wing: The queenside a-, b-, and c-files; or the kingside f-, g-, and h-files. Also called flank.

### Wing Gambit
`wikipedia:glossary:Wing Gambit` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Wing Gambit: The name given to variations of several openings in which one player gambits a wing pawn, usually the b-pawn.

### winning percentage
`wikipedia:glossary:winning percentage` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

winning percentage: A number calculated by adding together the number of games won and half of the number of games drawn (i.e. ignoring the losses), then dividing that total by the total number of games that were played. Another way of calculating the winning percentage is by taking the percentage of games won by a player plus half the percentage of drawn games. Thus, if out of 100 games a player wins 40 percent, draws 32 percent, and loses 28 percent, the winning percentage is 40 plus half of 32, i.e. 56 percent.

### winning position
`wikipedia:glossary:winning position` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

winning position: A position is said to be a winning one if one specified side, with correct play, can eventually force a checkmate against any defense (i.e. perfect defense). Also called won game.

### Woman Candidate Master ("WCM")
`wikipedia:glossary:Woman Candidate Master ("WCM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Woman Candidate Master ("WCM"): A women-only chess title ranking below Woman FIDE Master.

### Woman FIDE Master ("WFM")
`wikipedia:glossary:Woman FIDE Master ("WFM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Woman FIDE Master ("WFM"): A women-only chess title ranking below Woman International Master.

### Woman Grandmaster ("WGM")
`wikipedia:glossary:Woman Grandmaster ("WGM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Woman Grandmaster ("WGM"): The highest ranking gender-restricted chess title except for Women's World Champion.

### Woman International Master ("WIM")
`wikipedia:glossary:Woman International Master ("WIM")` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Woman International Master ("WIM"): A women-only chess title ranking below Woman Grandmaster and above Woman FIDE Master.

### wood
`wikipedia:glossary:wood` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

wood: [slang] Pieces. "A lot of wood came off the board" conveys that several piece exchanges occurred.

### woodpusher
`wikipedia:glossary:woodpusher` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

woodpusher: [colloq., typically derogatory] A weak chess player, also referred to as a patzer or duffer. See also fish.

### World Champion
`wikipedia:glossary:World Champion` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

World Champion: A winner of the World Chess Championship.

### wrong bishop
`wikipedia:glossary:wrong bishop` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

wrong bishop: Or wrong-colored bishop. A bishop that, because of the color squares it is restricted to, suffers critical loss of utility in the game position. See also wrong rook pawn.

### wrong rook pawn
`wikipedia:glossary:wrong rook pawn` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

wrong rook pawn: With a bishop, a rook pawn may be the wrong rook pawn, depending on whether or not the bishop controls its promotion square.

### X-ray
`wikipedia:glossary:X-ray` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

X-ray: When the power of a piece, either to attack or to defend, seems to pass through an intervening enemy piece. An X-ray attack, also known as a skewer, occurs when two pieces of the same color are caught in the same line of attack along a diagonal, rank, or file. The attacking piece forces the first and more valuable piece to move out of the way, which allows the second piece to be captured. An X-ray defense occurs when one piece is defended by another piece through an attacking enemy piece standing between the two.

### zeitnot
`wikipedia:glossary:zeitnot` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

zeitnot: [from German, 'time need'] Having very little time on the clock to complete the remaining moves of a timed game. Also called time pressure and time trouble. See also time control.

### Zonal tournaments
`wikipedia:glossary:Zonal tournaments` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

Zonal tournaments: Tournaments organized by FIDE, the first qualifying cycle of the World Chess Championship. Each zonal tournament features top players of a certain geographical zone. Up until 1993 the winners went on to Interzonal tournaments. This was replaced by a system where the winners now play each other in knockout-style competitions to determine who goes on to the Candidates Tournament.

### zugzwang
`wikipedia:glossary:zugzwang` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

zugzwang: [from German, 'compulsion to move'] When a player is put at a disadvantage by having to make a move; where any legal move weakens the position. Zugzwang usually occurs in the endgame, and rarely in the middlegame.

### zwischenschach
`wikipedia:glossary:zwischenschach` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

zwischenschach: [from German, 'in-between check'] Playing a surprising check that the opponent did not consider when plotting a sequence of moves; a zwischenzug that is a check.

### zwischenzug
`wikipedia:glossary:zwischenzug` · [source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_chess)

zwischenzug: [from German, 'in-between move'] An "in-between" move, or an intermezzo, played before an expected reply. Often, but not always, this involves responding to a threat by posing an even greater threat, forcing the opponent to respond to the threat first.
